ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u2	Dear Members and honourable Members, distinguished representatives of the Government, welcome! We have also prepared a structural review of developments in the economic, social and sustainable field at the Office for Macroeconomic Analysis this year, and I will continue to present you with key findings on the most important macroeconomic developments in Slovenia. A key conclusion of our analysis in the field of economic development is that the development of Slovenia, which we have witnessed in the last 15 years, has ceased during the period of crisis. In 2008, 2009 we came close to the average development of the euro area at all of the 9% points, and at the time of the crisis we began to distance ourselves and the latest data show that we are on average falling behind about 15%. In other words, we have returned to the situation we recorded somewhere between 2003 and 2004. What are the reasons for this developmental setback? It's basically two groups of factors. On the one hand, Slovenia still retains relatively good results with regard to the employment rate, and on the other hand, we have great problems with productivity. On average, only 60% of the average productivity of the European Union is achieved throughout the economy, and at this point in Slovenia there is not a single economic sector that achieves the average of this productivity, much less to get close to those countries that are the best, outliers and with whom we want to compare. What are the key reasons for this lack of productivity? If I just count the most important. It is the fact that, at this point in Slovenia, we still have a very low share of industry in the structure of the economy, which can be described as technologically difficult, medium and highly demanding, that we also have low productivity in the high-tech sector segment. While we have a relatively satisfactory level of investment in research and development, we do not benefit very well from these funds. If I give you only one comparison, Slovenia registers about 60 patents per million inhabitants. The European Union averages 120 patents per million inhabitants. The next thing that threatens our potential to increase productivity is inefficiency in the education system, especially in tertiary education. The surveys conducted throughout the European Union show that the proportion of employers not satisfied with the skills of graduates in Slovenia is extremely high. There is virtually only one country across the European Union where this share is higher, with 18% of all employers with the skills of graduates dissatisfied in Slovenia, with an average of 8%. In the most successful countries, for example Germany, Denmark, Norway, this share is only 3%. The next thing that threatens our productivity is the rise in real labour costs. Due to measures taken in the past, both to raise the minimum wage and to pay the prizes in the economy even after the economic results have already deteriorated, it has led to an increase of more than 6% in labour per unit of product in Slovenia at the moment, while on average the European Union is increasing by only 1%. This comparison therefore shows a six-fold deterioration in Slovenia's position, as on average in the European Union. In addition, in Slovenia we are still witnessing the country's high role in the economy and also in the economy, which is, of course, linked to the inefficient management of businesses. In addition to these problems, which hinder our ability to raise productivity, we also have problems with financing the country and businesses. In recent years, the budget deficit has increased significantly. As you can see from this chart before you, we have practically covered structural deficits over the last 15 years, that is, the deficit created by economic policies, by covering up the positive economic results before 2008 and the actual deficit was small or we even had a balanced budget in 2007, and after the outbreak of the crisis the public financial situation deteriorated greatly, and this has led to difficult access to funding sources for Slovenia. At the moment, the Republic of Slovenia is practically very difficult to commit to international markets. If we look at what happened in the past when we had economic growth, which was somewhere between four and five percent, Slovenia was in debt at a lower interest rate, the interest rate was less than 5%, now that we are witnessing a decline in economic activity, or when we expect it to be actively reduced by about 1% this year, our debt price is slightly more than 5%, which is of course an unsustainable situation in this macroeconomic aspect. As a result, this situation in the area of country debt is also transferred to the banking sector, our banks are therefore more difficult to borrow in international markets, and this difficult access to funding resources is an important factor in why they have a larger decline in economic activity in Slovenia than other members of the euro area or why we expect even worse economic results. If we add to all these factors that inhibit our growth, low foreign investment, which, on the one hand, impede the transfer of knowledge to Slovenia and, on the other hand, hinder the transfer of new resources to Slovenia, then we can find that Slovenia has lost its market share in international markets in recent years. The comparison shows that at this point we are following the latest data where we were about 10 years ago, this is in 2010, 2011. Our market share increased gradually before the crisis by 2008 and began to decline during the crisis, meaning, in other words, that our competitors, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Poland, that is, the countries with which we compete in international markets, are slowly moving away from these markets, that is, distance from the economic objective. If I continue with social development or with the achievements that we record in the labour market and in social systems, then there has also been a shift to the objectives set or to a major deterioration in the situation during the crisis. This is where we record results that we have not been used to in the past 10, 15 years. Slovenia has gradually approached the European Union in recent years. Gradually, material living conditions and the well - being of the population improved. However, this crisis is a development which has also disrupted this process in the social field. First, I would like to start with the worsening situation in the labour market. We all know that unemployment has increased significantly during the crisis, that employment has decreased, but with these poor data, labour market structures have also deteriorated. Here I would only mention that, for example, the work activity of older people has increased significantly, from 31% two years ago to as much as 35%, to double the long-term unemployment rate, for example, in 2009 it was 1.7%, with the latest figures of 3.5%, and there was also a strong segregation in the labour market. We have two problems. When we look at what is happening to youth employment, then Slovenia, in international comparisons, is back on track, unfortunately in a negative sense. In Slovenia we have practically the largest share of temporary employment among young people. If we look at this age segment between 15 and 24 years old, in Slovenia, about 80% of this population has fixed-term employment, so it has short-term contracts, while on average the European Union has half the figure, is only 40%, and in those countries that are the most successful, it has only 10% of the young contracts for a certain period, i.e. the problem on the young side. On the other hand, we also have a problem with the older population. If we look at the work activity of those between the ages of 55 and 64, only 30% of the population is active in Slovenia, while the average in the EU is 50%. In those countries that are most successful, however, it reaches 60%. When we add all this together, we find that we have a relatively short period in Slovenia when we are working. Compared with the OECD countries, this period is practically the shortest, and of course it has consequences both for the satisfaction and well-being of the population and for public finances. When we sum up all these factors, then we find that in recent years the real available income, that is, the income that we have in Slovenia has begun to fall in real terms. Over the past 10 years and from 2000 to 2008, each year has been realistic, so if we clean up inflation, it has increased somewhere between 2.5% and 3%, and the crisis has led to a reversal of this trend, leading to a shift in this trend. Over the last three years, in 2009, 2010, 2011, this available income decreased by 3.4%. We at Umar estimate that this year will also see a reduction. And when we add up all these four years, we're going to figure out that real disposable income has gone down by 5%. If we add about 8% of the inflation that was in this period, then we now have nominally as much as 13% less resources for our consumption. The second problem I would like to mention in the social field is population ageing. Virtually all countries in the European Union are faced with the fact that their population is old, but all countries are not in the same position. If we compare ourselves with the other countries of the European Union, then we find that this problem of ageing in Slovenia is among the most pronounced. If we look at what demographic projections show next year, then we can see that important changes will take place in Slovenia in the next 15 or 20 years. The share of the active population, i.e. in the age group between 15 and 64, will decrease by between 10% and 15% in this period, i.e. fewer people will be employed, fewer people will be active and, on the other hand, the proportion of older people will increase, over 60 by about one half and over 80 years will increase in the next decades almost four times. This will, of course, lead to additional pressure on public finances. Here on this graph, if you look at it, you can see that expenditure related to social systems, the background of which is also population ageing, will increase by more than 1.5 percentage points over 15 to 20 years. This may not seem to be much at first sight, but if we think that Slovenia has spent only slightly more than 1.1% of its research and development resources in Slovenia over the past years, then this figure is placed in a completely different context and it is clear that it is a very big change. In this social field, however, we also have some indicators showing a favourable picture in Slovenia. Above all, the quality of life indicators tend to remain favourable or even improve. We still have a low risk of poverty in Slovenia, and material deprivation is relatively low when compared with other European Union countries. We maintain favourable accessibility to public services and some indicators, which have traditionally been beneficial in Slovenia, such as the low rate of infant mortality and the long-awaited life expectancy, are maintained at these relatively high levels. If I summarize this event briefly, these changes that have occurred in recent years in Slovenia, then we can find that in many indicators or in many indicators at this point where Slovenia was already in 2005, sometimes even in 2004, and in some cases we have returned to the situation that we have already achieved in 2002, which means, in other words, that international comparisons of Slovenia, in particular with other European countries, show that in many cases we are on the tail of these comparisons. This means that the years since or after the outbreak of the crisis have been lost from a development perspective for Slovenia. What happened? Slovenia had a potential for economic growth sometime between 3.5 and 4% before the crisis. This means that we were able to achieve an average of 3.5% economic growth without creating greater imbalances in the Slovenian economic system, i.e. without excessive indebtedness, without excessive external trade surpluses, without excessive inflation. After the outbreak of the crisis, this potential has declined significantly, more than halved, and at this point in the Office we estimate that Slovenia is able to achieve economic growth of less than 1% in the coming years. Our forecast for this year even speaks of economic activity shrinking by about 0.9% this year. Finally, I would like to make a few words of recommendations or areas that we believe should be addressed if we are to avoid or get out of this trap of low or even negative economic growth. I'm sure there's public finances first. At the moment, in Slovenia, the level of expenditure is such that the economy cannot finance it without borrowing abroad. However, since this debt abroad for Slovenia is now severely hampered by the fact that the indicative yields on our bonds are such that we cannot afford them from a macroeconomic point of view, this suggests the necessity of fiscal consolidation. It is necessary to take measures to rationalise the current expenditure, to rewind individual expenditure groups, but that will not be enough. If we have in mind the demographic picture of the Slovenian population, if we have in mind the expected pressures that ageing will have on public finances, then in the medium term public finances cannot be balanced without updating social protection systems. This is where the pension system comes first, and then the health system and the long-term care system. These changes in social systems are not only necessary to balance public finances, but also to allow those generations who will retire in the coming years at least the same standard or at least the same conditions as those of generations or people who have retired in the past. So public finances are first, followed by the modernisation of social protection systems and the third major area that we will have to address in Slovenia to restore economic growth is the area of competitiveness. Slovenia is a small country and if we compare our exports with gross domestic product, this comparison is 70 %. Slovenia is crucially dependent on how we manage to position ourselves in foreign markets, how we can get into foreign markets and then stay in foreign markets. And if we want to achieve this in order to reverse this negative trend when our market share is declining, then the competitiveness of the Slovenian economy will need to be increased. And one last thing. The great problems in the labour market and these structural problems show that our labour market is very rigid, that we have problems with the transition from unemployment to employment, that then this group of people who are employed is very protected, which is therefore causing problems in business decisions. I therefore think that, after all, some attention should also be paid to greater flexibility in the labour market. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u4	Thank you for the floor, Mr President. Dear Members, distinguished citizens and citizens! The National Assembly is today and tomorrow before one of the most important decisions in its history. The step we are taking today has been promised by the previous government to our European partners already in 2010, and we are the last in the European Union to try to take this step. However, we are working this step primarily because of ourselves and because of our own future. This step is not simple, it has not been anywhere, nor has it been with us. Slovenia is particularly concerned with this step also with a psychological problem and a problem – Slovenia has progressed since independence. From 1991 to 2008, we were ahead of average developments in the European Union. Slovenia came from a poor 45% of the European environment's average development to 91% in 2008. We almost captured Italy in 18 years, which was for half the developmental step ahead of us at the beginning of our independent life. In 2008, we were more affected by the crisis than others. And since 2008 Slovenia has not been catching the European average, but has been lagging behind. According to the latest data, we have fallen by more than 5 percentage points, destroying the advantage we had before all the new Member States of the European Union at the beginning of this crisis. Despite the fact that the crisis has affected us, despite the fact that we have lost 60,000 jobs at home at that time, so many fewer of our citizens today have been working in the Slovenian economy after three and a half years, despite the fact that some 60,000 foreign citizens are working in Slovenia today than at the beginning of the crisis, and despite the severe shock that the economy has experienced in this crisis, that more than 12,000 businesses and entrepreneurs have lost their jobs at that time, the majority of the consequences of this crisis on the standard, on the public sector, on the scale of public sector services in Slovenia have been mitigated by debt over the past three years. Thus, if we can say that by 2008 we had been about the same in Slovenia as possible, receiving growth in prosperity, that Slovenia had progressed, that wages had gone up, that pensions had gone up, that the standard had increased, that employment had also increased, that more and more people could be educated and so on, and since 2008 this picture has been shared. On the one hand, the effects of the crisis, which were very familiar in the economy, the tens of thousands of unemployed people who felt this crisis most and their families, their family, all those who depended on them, which, of course, increased the pressure on social transfers, depending on the guaranteed or guaranteed rights, and on the other hand the public sector, which had not adapted or adapted to the shock or impact of the crisis at that time. As you know, in 2010, there was a consensus that the public sector's wages did not increase so much, so partial elimination of wage imbalances was frozen. But wages, unlike the economy, in the public sector remained at a real pre-crisis level or even increased. At a time when we lost nearly 120,000 new jobs in the economy, almost 10,000 people were employed in the public sector. Part of these jobs were justified because they were real needs and were part of the public sector. However, some of these jobs were not justified during the crisis. This led to scissors, which were mentioned earlier by the Director of the Government's Office for Macroeconomic Analysis and Development. When we talk about the psychological problem, we also talk about the problems that arise whenever, for the first time in history, some measures need to be coordinated, when indices do not increase, when numbers in the budget or in the balance do not increase, when expectations need to be limited, and when certain things need to be reduced. In fact, the majority of the generation facing these coordinations, negotiation and intervention does not even have this experience. And despite the fact that there is still a lot of memory about inflationary times in the former country and problems at that time, it is human nature to forget such things quickly, to remember more beautiful things, and in some ways then high expectations and memories of times when things went up, always prevail. So, apart from the public finances, it is also a psychological problem and we are facing it today. The adoption of the budget rebalancing for this year, which will soon be half past, is only a first step, one of the three urgent steps for Slovenia to emerge from the crisis, to re-enter the path of sustainable growth and prosperity. The balance of the budget that you have before you is essentially a measure of consolidation – of course with all the accompanying acts – of Slovenian public finances. That's the first step. The second step is regaining economic growth and the third step is new employment or new jobs in the economy. All these three steps are connected. This isn't about whether or not, it's not about three choices, it's about the three items we have on the table, and now we're going to take the one we love. This is a structural link between all these measures and these are also the consequences. And it's not a theory, it's also a practice that we can follow in a number of other European countries, we can compare these steps, we can compare the effects of these steps, and we can see where the countries that have taken this seriously, and where the countries where people have pressed or those who should make decisions have pushed their heads into the sand. Without consolidation of public finances in the context of a modern economy and a single European market to which Slovenia belongs, it is not possible to relaunch sustainable economic growth. And without economic growth it is not possible to expect new jobs in the economy. Until there are new jobs, it is not possible to strengthen the social function of the country, as real growth in prosperity can only be financed by real economic growth or by increasing what we create. When we talk about consolidation of public finances, the reduction of the budget deficit is one of the main objectives of this budget rebalancing, a key measure. Slovenia has reached almost or more than 6% of the budget deficit in the last three years on average. Our partners, who are bound by the Stability and Growth Pact as well as other rules in the euro area, because Slovenia is not only a member of the European Union, are also a member of the European Monetary Union, and these rules now set significantly different borders. When it comes to budget deficits, not only did we exceed the three per cent limit of the current Stability and Growth Pact for a few percent, but we exceeded it by 100 per cent points, for three years, at the same time greatly increased our debt, which was the second smallest in the euro area at the beginning of the crisis. Over the last three years, we have had the highest increase in indebtedness in the European Union. Without a reduction of the budget deficit, there is no consolidation of public finances. Here we are not just talking about measures to reduce spending on the expenditure side of the budget, we are talking about a comprehensive set, because only here is the solution. And also in this law to balance public finances, which you will deal with under the next item, there are measures on both sides. However, once again, we are talking about the 2012 budget rebalancing and we are accepting it for the 5th month, the year is 12 months, we are accepting it for the 5th month of the year. He'll be established if there's no complications in the middle of the year. And this fact only speaks of the fact that we are substantially limited in reducing the budget deficit by measures on the revenue side of the budget, because it is mainly measures that cannot be implemented overnight, which are largely possible to apply at the beginning of the following year. Most of this debate is coming soon, in a few months, when you will have to consider the draft budgets for the next two years. If anyone is concerned that the focus is mainly on the revenue side of the budget, he will be greatly comforted this year because many of these measures will still be on the table. I hope so. When it comes to borrowing, Slovenia has reached its limits. The criteria of the European Stability and Growth Pact, however, set a debt limit to which there is no penalty and which is acceptable to 60% of GDP. We're some bad 10% points today, below this limit, but we'll reach it before we even the budget. All these measures, which are ahead of us, do not address the deficit, they only reduce it, and we still need the debt to be around the normal functioning. We have received a number of comments on these budget documents with an argument or demand that consumption be increased and with an argument that we can still be indebted because we are not yet so in charge. We're on the cutting edge if we want to get out of this situation without major shocks. If we were already at 60% today, the debt should be stopped immediately and then it would actually be drastic measures. Besides, he's in debt, he's in debt, he's in debt, he's in interest. You are more in debt and poorer structural expectations are in public finances to move forward, it is more expensive to continue to be in debt. Of course, these things are connected. One of the approaches to the preparation of the budget rebalancing and accompanying acts is the government's desire to distribute these burdens fairly. This is your aim between a few dozen proposals or measures, proposals for crisis benefits and crisis taxes, which concern the more tangible parts of Slovenian society. We establish a safety net or censorship in measures so that reductions do not affect those living on a minimum wage, with a lower pension, etc. This is a very sensitive social approach, which we have not seen in any other European country where such measures have been taken, and we have examined practically everything. In this debate, also due to the simultaneous process of the Constitutional Commission, additional debate and integration with the budgetary debate on the golden fiscal rule were opened. As far as the golden fiscal rule is concerned, or the goal of balancing our budget so that we can spend as much as we create or gather in it in the future, this objective was promised to our partners in the common monetary union and the European Union as early as 2010. It's not something this government invented. The negotiations on the fiscal pact have also been concluded before the beginning of this mandate, and you have in fact unanimously ratified the fiscal pact, which requires us to introduce the golden fiscal rule into the internal legal order already last month. This golden fiscal rule is primarily the protection of Slovenian taxpayers from adventurous moves either by the domestic executive authorities or from the conduct of other countries with which we share the same currency. So all these debates that appear about whether this is even necessary and so on, or not for the benefit of the rich and so on, are completely depressed. If anything, then at a time when the European Union is faced with irresponsible behaviour in some Member States, and these irresponsible behaviours are also paid by our taxpayers, our interest is that this is no longer the case. And there is no Slovenian citizen who would be pleased that he must also guarantee Greek debts. By introducing this golden fiscal rule, we are introducing rules of the game, which, of course, will also be sanctioned, which will make it impossible to spend or live at the expense of others in the European family. Against the golden fiscal rule, there can only be someone who doesn't understand what's going on or someone who has some interests. In any event, the golden rule is primarily the protection of taxpayers, the ordinary taxpayer, who is protected by this rule from excessive risks, while at the same time the golden rule is also the protection of future generations, so that even in some way a irresponsible generation does not owe them a more critical time. For this reason, I will say, all honour to the Constitutional Commission, which resumed the procedure yesterday, and I hope that the change of the constitutional article that introduces this golden fiscal rule will soon be in the Slovenian Constitution that this is a huge step that the Slovenian National Assembly will take at this time and will also be a strong signal outside that directly influences the improvement of our international financial position. If anyone hoped that the policy in the European Union would temporarily change, that this was no longer the case after these Sunday elections in Greece and France, of course he made a serious mistake. Just because there's no other way. There's no other way. The fiscal pact will enter into force in a relatively short period of time, the ratification of 12 countries is sufficient and we are already halfway there. And there will be no new negotiation. This is also the position of the European Commission. Yesterday you could also hear a statement by President Schulz of the European Parliament, or socialist Schulz, who is in the same position as most other Member States, after these communications in the last days against any opening already agreed. But where would we have come if, after each election, we had changed what had already been agreed upon or signed in each country? And in some countries it's already ratified. In what position would you be placed as the legislative authority in this country that you have already ratified the fiscal pact, by consensus, in fact, if someone in Parliament now said, "Yes, but we had an election, don't hold what the previous government has signed, let's go to new negotiations." This would indeed be the desavation of democracy and the beginning of the collapse of the rules of the game, on which the European Union, as well as the European monetary union, are at all fundamental. It is also important to know that most Greek debts are no longer on private commercial banks. For Greek debts you guarantee, taxpayers all over Europe guarantee, these are public funds and we have reached the limits when we can still risk them. Rather than any change in the rules of the game from what has been agreed, it is likely that the euro area, Greece's departure or those countries that are unable to comply with the rules of the game will be reduced. And from this, Slovenia must also draw its lesson. The interpretations that spread both across Europe and with us on how Greek voters punished those who proposed drastic austerity measures and reached an agreement with the European Union and the International Monetary Fund on austerity measures are only part of the truth. The Greek voters also punished those political elites who, through excessive spending and spending, brought the country into such a situation. Any other interpretation would be a great underestimation of the generation of voters, which at some fundamental level always exists. The parties most lost ruled decades ago, with an absolute majority, one, one, one, the other, sending the wrong data to the European Statistical Office and allowing what happened to happen to them by fraud. Speaking of consolidation of public finances as a prerequisite for new economic growth, we can talk about a structural link or a direct financial link; perhaps we should start by making this more understandable and very illustrative. You already have a huge number in this budget balance sheet, I think 750 million euros, or around here, that Slovenia will pay this year for interest on our debts. This number is significantly larger than it was in last year and is significantly larger than it was in the pre-Last year. If you compare this number with the so-called saved sum, which is the wrong label, but it is said so, then you see that everything we save by reducing wages, reducing travel costs and in all other areas, we will pay interest on the debts we have created. None of this, unfortunately, can go to investment. And if we continue this path, of course every year it will be more interest, because it is debt to pay, and it will be even less for anything else. We're on the edge now. Thus, the reduction in debt, the reduction of this pressure on the financing costs or the servicing of our debts is directly linked to the amount of resources available to us for investment, both public and in the economy. This is also linked in terms of duties and burdens and so on. And this connection needs to be seen. The creation of a more favourable environment for domestic and foreign entrepreneurs, the decommissioning of work, flexicurity is as important as tackling this pressure. Today, those European countries, Slovenia, are among them that have not done so, of course, are beating. Spain also has record unemployment in Europe because Spanish companies move their production to Slovakia, the Czech Republic and so on because of the rigid labour market. And they're in the same position as us, by having a bigger economy. For new economic growth, foreign investment, productive use of European funds, including more productive use of state capital investments, are essential. These are the steps that await us in the future. The burden of work, the reward of good work, innovation, this is the path to be taken in contrast with these proposals in order to further increase the balance sheet. There is no way out of the economic crisis in the balance sheet. Those who do more and better work, which is more innovative, must also be better rewarded. Not only in the economy, but also in the public sector. Therefore, in these negotiations with the public sector unions, the government has also proposed that wage imbalances, which are criminal to those who work better and create more, be eliminated. And we went into it, and I hope that this deal is finally signed. When we talk about what is important for economic growth, or when, not only in us, but also in general in the European area, this controversy is now taking place, whether it is savings or growth, it should be reiterated that this is an artificial dilemma, without consolidating public finances, to reduce the deficit in order to also ensure the strength of the common currency, there is no condition for sustainable growth. The fact that this dilemma is artificial also speaks of a number of documents that the European Union has drawn up in these times of crisis. The French elections did not bring debate on the need for economic growth. Last October, the European Commission presented a document detailing what measures should be taken, and it is already accepted that economic growth is being relaunched in Europe, but public financial consolidation is a prerequisite. Yesterday on Shuman's Day, or Europe's Day, the European Commission published a Communication entitled Time for Decision, Growth Action, Action for New Jobs, where it once again represents this link between the necessity of consolidating public finances in the European Union, especially in the euro area, and economic growth. These policies exist, Slovenia has co-formed them in their creation in recent years, and these agreements need to be adhered to, and they are also entirely logical decisions. As we speak of the third pillar or new employment in the economy, it is now clear that economic growth, the new investments alone can promote employment in the economy, is no other productive path. Here we have serious negotiations with the social partners, this second step in the preparation of a social agreement, where we will have to agree on this. It's not a disaster if someone loses a job, it's a disaster if you lose a job and don't get the same or better paid job right away in real time. And if, for a short time, when you are unemployed, there is no safety net, we have this safety net, the unemployment benefits in Slovenia are significantly lower than the minimum wage. Even now, these conditions are not getting worse. On the other hand, however, we have done nothing for flexibility. This is one of the main obstacles to new investment and also attracting foreign investment. Two days ago, I was shown a place where the previous German government, led by Chancellor Schroeder, was preparing the 2010 Agenda. This was at a time before the crisis when Germany had over 5 million, historically large numbers, unemployed. I must say that it went back to the times when unions and many others in Germany protested the introduction of the so-called flexible labour market. This market has been introduced. And today, despite the crisis in the developed world, Germany has a record low unemployment rate. Every morning, if you go after Presherno, probably a lot of MPs go by, you see species. Even this morning, there were over a hundred people lined up before the German consulate, where they were waiting for a work visa to work in Germany. These are people who used to work in Slovenia. It's important to ask why these species are there. One of the reasons is that we have not made the so-called labour market flexible enough and that remains for us if we do not want to share the fate of those on the path of destruction. The third extremely important thing mentioned earlier in Dr. Vaslet's performance is the restructuring of our school programs if you want to reform education. Every school in Slovenia also needs to analyse where its graduates are, how many of these people have received employment in their profession, how many are unemployed, how many have had to be trained. The vast majority of our educational institutions do not work or are not real. When we visit Slovenian companies today, at a time when we have more than 110 000 unemployed, we encounter examples of companies looking for workers, but there are no such profiles, they must be trained and so on. The huge gap is between the profiles produced by our educational system and what we need today and what we will need for the next ten years. The huge gap is between this production and what the European labour market will need in the next ten years. We have dozens of institutes that have calculated all this, who know that, who have got the money and are paid for it, but nothing has done to adapt to it. But it's going to have to be done. If I'm trying to narrow it down to support the budget rebalancing. The step we take is not simple, but necessary. We are the last in the European Union to do this. It's no easier way than what we do. There are many false prophets who invite on their hearts as mermaid sailors. But we know how such a journey ends. May our example be those who are successful, not those who avoid problems. We have both cases, we don't have to go far around the world to find them. And the smart ones imitate the good and the successful, not the ones that aren't. So it is no easier way, Slovenia has lived for three years in the belief that things will improve in themselves, that everything will be fine once Germany grows again. Germany is growing, and it has been accelerating for two years now, and things have not quite settled in themselves. We're in a recession again, and I think there must be an end to the utopian belief that even if we wanted to, we could not, and that someone else would pay our bills. Maybe some of the real bases for this utopia existed by the end of last year, and then came the sobering of 7% of the profit of our ten-year bond, which is a sign for all the alarm bells. I don't think there should be any utopian since then. Today we are taking the first step, before the next steps we will also have to take very difficult measures. As this first step continues with the preparation of the budget for the next two years, we are awaiting negotiations with the social partners for a new social agreement. Despite all the complications, efforts, misunderstandings, I think we are close to having the vast majority of the social partners in the Economic and Social Council finally sign the starting points for a new social agreement. Many have already done so. And there it is necessary to agree on concrete, real and even fair steps in all that needs to be done to ensure that Slovenia starts to grow again: in labour market reform, the pension system, health reform and, in particular, in adapting what we are financing from public money to real opportunities. We can be at least partially optimistic here, and the next few weeks will show how quickly we can make progress through a social agreement. The government would love it very much, and I could say that the government coalition would very much like the opposition to accept the partnership's proposed hand. We've been waiting about a month and a half for a single letter of answers to our offer. We have proposed both a substantive framework and a cooperation protocol. There has been no interest so far, nor has there been any comment. However, I must say that our doors are still open, today's decision-making is not over, it is only the first smaller step, and I hope that there will be different views on this side and that it will be possible to agree. On this occasion, I would also like to thank the social partners with whom we have negotiated over the past few weeks, first of all, for the fact that we have at least approximated the assessment of the situation in which Slovenia is and then for some of the first measures we are now taking. There is no perfect unity and it would also be illusory to expect it, but I think there are still many things that have been clarified, that we know where we are, that many things are also harmonised, some have also been signed, and that this is still a solid basis for us to move forward with the social agreement. ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u5	I thank the President of the Government for presenting the balance sheet, and I also give the floor to the Minister of Finance, Dr. Janez Šuštersič.
ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u6	Your Honor! Allow me to present myself briefly some of the basic characteristics of the balance sheet and the accompanying measures in the Public Finance Balance Act, which in fact allows for the adoption of such a balance. We came from two key numbers when preparing the rebalancing. One is an income assessment, saying how much, in the main existing system, the burden the economy generates to finance public spending. This figure is around €8 billion, and we have come from the goal of reducing the deficit of the state budget to around €9 billion in gross domestic product or in the amount of €1 billion this year, which has also placed a ceiling of around €9 billion. This figure, €9 billion in expenditure, is more than a billion lower than that foreseen by the budget still in force today, and this figure, as regards the income assessment that is real this year, is more than half a billion lower than that to which it is calculated in the preparation of the balance that is still in force. One reason is the assessment that this year's gross domestic product will be about 4.5 billion lower than what was calculated at the time the budget was being prepared this year. In view of last year's realisation, we also anticipate less expenditure than was realised last year for a poor EUR 400 million. If we add to this all that has not been or is not to the same extent in last year's realisation, it is necessary this year, say higher interest costs, to come together that we had to reduce the actual expenditure by around €800 million by various measures compared to last year. These are the basic numbers. How did we do that? First of all, by starting serious processes, rationalising cost-effective behaviour. The government has adopted a number of conclusions, virtually every week in this area, we went through the budget together with the ministers and the minister, checked the programmes of various policies, investments, and tried everything that we did not feel absolutely necessary to adapt. However, it was not possible to achieve this objective alone, and we have also decided to amend all the legislation on which the expenditure of the state budget and public treasury are based. We've done this in several rounds of conversation. All these changes are now covered by the Public Finance Balance Act. As an important part of public expenditure is also expenditure on labour market policies, on family policy, on social policy, we also had to propose certain adjustments in the rules setting out these expenditure. Without this simple objective, we could not have achieved it. Similarly, by amending legislation or other regulations, we had to propose significant adjustments in the functioning of the public sector also in the area of payments for the work of those working in the public sector, or in compensation for other labour-related costs. That was an emergency, too. We also felt justified, given that in recent years the adjustment to the crisis has been significantly greater in the private sector than in the public sector. These measures, of course, are not simple. We are aware that they will affect a lot of people, and it was not easy to propose, nor simply to defend them. Even at a completely personal level, we have often had to look people in the eye and convince them that we actually believe that this is the only option we have in Slovenia today. Why must these steps of balancing public finances be hasty? Because data for previous years show that the deficit has not begun to decline in Slovenia in the last three years. However, we all know that this week the European Commission will also make its assessment of whether Slovenia is on track to fulfil those commitments that we have made as a country. The key of these commitments is that in the coming year, in 2013, the deficit of the whole public sector will be below 3% of gross domestic product, which means more than half as low as it was last year. Therefore, we simply cannot wait for the budget to be drawn up for next year, but we have already had to propose with this rebalancing measures to enable us to draw convincingly closer to this goal that we have to achieve next year. Such a planned deficit in the country's budget, which is foreseen by this rebalancing, is 3% of gross domestic product, will also allow us to see the deficit of the whole country sector and the whole public sector somewhere between 3% and 4% of gross domestic product. And it will allow us to plan the budget next year by meeting this commitment we have made. Perhaps at the beginning, because we actually had very little time not only to prepare the measures, but also to present them, there will be some minor differences in the numbers that we will be talking about here and we will officially accept them as our goal, and those that will be the first assessments by the European Commission, but, for us, what is essential is, above all, the substantive assessment that we are succeeding with these measures and in a different way of working to turn Slovenian public financial policy in the right direction and that we are on the right track to fulfil our commitments within this deadline. The question that often arises in relation to these measures is whether they will affect economic growth. This question is raised in the context of thinking, which in my opinion is inappropriate or does not correspond to the situation in which Slovenia is today, does not correspond to the options that Slovenian economic policy has today. We simply cannot think about increasing the deficit or maintaining the same deficit, and this is the means of borrowing to accelerate economic growth for a number of reasons. Firstly, because experience over the past years has shown that such a policy in a small open economy does not produce the right results. We already have three years of high deficits, and we still have about 0% economic growth and we have a growing number of unemployed people. At the same time, we believe that the key word needed to follow is to enable a new investment momentum, credibility, the credibility of economic policy and Slovenia as an investment environment. I do not believe that the budget could drive economic growth, at least not the main driver of economic growth. The budget must be prepared both on the income and on the expenditure side, that it is sustainable and that it is encouraging for those wishing to invest in a country, both domestic and foreign. This battle for credibility has been lost in recent years. I could have told you a lot of stories that I had heard in recent weeks by people, especially from abroad, as well as from local people who wanted to invest in Slovenia, but for that there was no proper hearing, received promises that were not fulfilled or that they ran into a wall of bureaucratic and other obstacles. We need to change these things and do much for our own conviction, or other projects waiting for the government, the start of the investment cycle, the recapitalisation of banks, without budget cooperation and the like, so it is imperative to make this fiscal adjustment today and make it convincing. The accounts presented to us by the government's official macroeconomic forecasters also talk about this. It is true that they have pointed out that this year's budget adjustment will have a slightly negative impact on economic growth by around 0.5% of GDP, but at the same time they have pointed out that without these measures it would only be worse in the coming years. While we can now count on positive economic growth in the coming years, if this fiscal balance is not proposed, up to 15 percent and more would have declined by 2015. This is the scenario we want to avoid with these measures. Since we, as a government, proposed these measures, the Proposal for a Law on Public Finance Balance and the Proposal for a Balance, intensive coordination has taken place with trade unions, the public, interest groups and we have also listened to certain warnings and proposals by the opposition. As a result of this negotiation, these discussions have been marked by changes. In the actions we have presented through the amendments in the past days at the meetings of the parent committee and even today at the meeting, we will do so. Let me mention something. In the area of social policy, labour market policies have changed the measures to abolish free kindergarten into a measure by which parents will contribute 30% of the costs to another child, depending on the class in which they are classified. Third and subsequent children, however, will continue to have a free kindergarten. Parental leave will continue to pay 90% and not 80% in recent months, as we originally proposed. The maximum level of parental leave will be limited to 2 times the average wage and not to 1.5. The unemployment allowance will remain the same in the first months as it is, and in the second year of unemployment we will reduce it to 50% in order to encourage job search and also reduce the duration in which the beneficiaries receive this compensation for the unemployed. May 2nd will continue to be a holiday. At the same time, following proposals made by trade unions and also by opposition parties, we have introduced into the very law on public finances a number of measures on the tax side that will facilitate the financing of fiscal adjustment and try to cover assets, capital that has not been sufficiently taxed so far. This is an additional tax on high-value real estate over the value of EUR 1 million. It is an additional taxation of powerful large expensive vehicles and vessels. This is about increasing the rate of income from capital from 20% to 25%, including the introduction of the fourth tax class in income, 50% in the amount of five average wages as a temporary anti-crisis tax for 2013 and 2014, and also the measure proposed in the law if we do not reach the goal of lowering the deficit of the state budget below 3% of the gross domestic product in 2014 automatically raising the rate of value added tax by as much as next year's surplus deficit, it is said that we have installed an automatic stabiliser. In the field of the public sector, in negotiations with the unions, we agreed to reduce wages by 10%, as provided for by law, to 8%. We have agreed that both steps to eliminate disproportionates should be eliminated immediately, at the same time as wage cuts, and some adjustments to some other work-related benefits. We have also agreed to move the debate on standards in education from this law to an agreement in the next few weeks. How will the financial effects of these adjustments be covered? With adjustments within the balance sheet with appropriate amendments. In order to be able to do so, we have proposed another measure in recent days, which was linked to the recognition of specific periods of retirement, periods of education, military service and so on. Given that we wanted to reach agreement with the social partners in particular, this measure was withdrawn yesterday, and we will have the means to cover these arrangements, the value of about 100 million, possibly several million, provided with transfers within the budget in the course of the year and with some amendments to be proposed today. Nevertheless, we will maintain the deficit of the state budget at that target level of one billion and 3% of the gross domestic product. What is the meaning of this agreement? Yesterday evening, we received a commitment, a promise, also by the public sector unions, to sign an agreement today. This signing is just taking place. What is the meaning of this arrangement for us? It is crucial that trade unions have given up the possibility that the Public Finance Balance Act itself will be subject to a referendum initiative or support such initiatives. This means that in the coming months, instead of dealing with the referendum, unstable, risky situations, we will have a peaceful situation in which the government will be able to focus on other measures that must also be taken. Trade unions will join those who have not yet signed the social agreements. It is very clear with them and it is also stated in these agreements that, in the framework of the talks on the social agreement to be concluded by summer, we continue to discuss pension reform, including those measures that have now been withdrawn, discussions on education standards, discussions on labour market reforms, all that is not in law now, and we will have to do as a state in the coming months and perhaps next year. At the end of the day, we still have a billion deficits left after this rebalancing, which will also need to be reduced in the coming years, because obviously there was enough consensus in the Constitutional Commission yesterday to move forward with the introduction of a fiscal rule into the constitution. This will also command us to find solutions for the remaining billion deficits in the coming years by 2015. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u8	President, thank you for your word. Greetings to all present! At its 19th urgent meeting, on 8 May 2012, the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy considered the proposal for a rebalancing of the budget of the Republic of Slovenia for 2012 submitted by the Government to the National Assembly for consideration. The Committee was informed of the views of interested working bodies, which took note of the proposal for a rebalancing and did not formulate and adopt their amendments. The opinion of the Council was forwarded to the Committee and the Committee was also informed of the letters of interest to the public. During the business term, qualified applicants tabled sixty-six amendments to the proposed balance. The initial explanation of the tabled balance was given by the Minister of Finance, first of all pointing to the reasons for his submission. A key issue for this year's rebalancing was how to achieve a fiscal target that would allow the planned deficit to fall below 3% of gross domestic product, or billions and 72 million euros. With regard to the expected deficit, Slovenia has made a commitment and is expected to be convincing below 3%. At the time of adoption of the budget, which is adopted for two years, the forecast for economic growth was different than it is now. The government's decision on the excessive balance is in the direction of reducing budget expenditure. The aim of the government is to reach an agreement in which key trade unions will renounce the proposal for a rebalancing and a law on public finances to be subject to a referendum judgment. The Director of the Office for Macroeconomic Analysis and Development briefly presented a general economic picture for 2012 in which the economic situation has changed. The rapporteurs of interested working bodies, namely the Committee on Foreign Policy, the Committee on Health and the Committee on Agriculture, Forestry, Food and the Environment, presented the opinions adopted at their meetings. In the debate on the proposed rebalancing, members of a committee of opposition parliamentary groups pointed out that the way in which the economic crisis and the reduction of the deficit in Slovenia were tackled by the Government did not take the right path, as the measures only affect the expenditure side of the budget, particularly in the area of education and social protection. The deficit is the difference between revenue and expenditure and it would therefore be appropriate to influence and take measures on both sides of the budget, as some other members of the European Union have done. They believe that the proposal for a rebalancing is incomplete and point out the problem of projections for the following years because they consider them not realistic. There are several right ways to promote economic growth and one could also be to apply fiscal policy as one of the pillars of economic policy or to raise the level of public investment in the energy, infrastructure and environment that could have a positive impact on economic growth. Domestic demand and not just saving should also be positively affected. The problem is that austerity measures interfere with the social protection of people. They also highlighted the important impact of the pension system, the long-term sustainability of public finances and the important role of Slovenian banks. In the debate on the tabled balance, members of a coalition group committee said that the proposal for a rebalancing was realistic and expected to have a positive impact on the public financial situation. The balance sheet and the Proposal for a Law on Public Finance Balance should be adopted quickly as almost half of 2012 already has passed and Slovenia is losing funds every day without measures. The Committee identified itself to the amendments tabled by qualified candidates tabled in accordance with Article 166 of the Rules of Procedure and supported in this context all amendments of the coalition groups, amendments of the Parliamentary Group of Positive Slovenia to the following users: District Court in Koper, Kranj, Ljubljana, Maribor, Murski Soboti, Novi Gorica, New Town, Krske, Ptuj, Slovenj Gradc and the District Court in Ljubljana. The Committee did not support any of the other amendments tabled and did not identify themselves to amendments not tabled in accordance with the Rules of Procedure.

ParlaMint-SI_2012-05-10-SDZ6-Izredna-13.ana.u10	Thank you for your word. Good-bye, everybody! In the Group of Social Democrats, we believe that the priorities for reducing the public financial deficit should be based on a balance between the means for development, social security and savings. Unfortunately, we find that this is not the case in the 2012 balance. The government carries the burden of the crisis to the medium layer, minimally burdens the assets, even burdens capital, drastic interference with universities, science and technology and the drastic reduction of public administration funds will be met by many investment start-up procedures. And as the most interesting investment – investments in rail infrastructure are back on the side track. Therefore, we will not support the 2012 balance of the budget in the Group of Social Democrats. The proposed austerity measures amounting to €818 million and the reduction of the structural deficit by 2% of GDP under the Stability Programme show a policy of the Government of Rapid Savings, which will reduce the public spending of the state budget by €350 million. In the Group of Social Democrats, we regret that the government did not choose for 2012 a prudent and gradual consolidation of public finances that would not have affected economic growth. Reducing the public spending of the state budget by 350 million in 2012 means that the state budget will contribute to a reduction in economic growth directly and indirectly in the amount of twice the reduction in public spending or by 2% of GDP. Reducing the deficit at the expense of public spending alone will thus stifle the economy. We also refer to the analysis of the International Monetary Fund on rapid savings, which poses a major threat to economic recovery. The International Monetary Fund has already concluded in its 2010 analysis that fiscal consolidation, equivalent to 1% of GDP, usually reduces GDP by about half a percent in two years and raises unemployment by about 0.3 percentage points and reduces domestic demand, consumption and investment by about 1%. From this we conclude that government measures reducing the structural deficit in 2012 by 2% of GDP will reduce the country's general sector deficit without taking one-off measures by 1.6% of GDP, i.e. from 5.1 in 2011 to 3.5% of GDP in 2012. On the basis of such consolidation of public finances in 2012, at 1.6% of GDP, economic growth will decrease by 0.8% of GDP and unemployment will increase by 0.48% and 4 thousand 800 people. In the Group of Social Democrats, we estimate that the continuation of the deficit reduction should also be based on an increase in the revenue of the State budget. In order to achieve this, we have already shown in the previous mandate when we wanted to reduce the grey economy. At that time, however, the ruling SDS party proposed a referendum on the Law on the Prevention of Work on the Black, which would bring employment for indefinite periods to employees in black, and a small job would also reduce the grey economy. The fall of pension reform has also increased the number of pensioners to record levels. As a result, the hole in 2012 is 300 million. As a result, there is a larger hole in the health treasury. In the Group of Social Democrats, we must stress that if our laws were to be adopted in the previous term of the referendum, public spending should not be so drastically reduced today in 2012, the intervention law should only be extended to freeze public sector wages by the end of 2012 and propose higher taxation of the richest. When we look at what the current government's income policy is, we find that the income scale has gone in the direction that, in the greatest possible way, is the richest. That is why, in our parliamentary group, we have proposed amendments and also an income law proposing an additional income class of €60,000 gross per year, which will tax the richest. The proposed additional class would increase the revenue of the state budget by around €40 million. This gives the government the opportunity to draw up more favourable austerity measures on the revenue side. We believe that the level of introduction of an additional income class at 3.2 times the average wage is an appropriate level of taxation because in fact it would be taxed on the richest people with wages above EUR 2 000 700 net, which is 25 000 in Slovenia. Unfortunately, we find that the government does not want to tax the richer. Although our proposal for an income class has been summarized, it has sought to tax those who have salaries higher than 5 times the average wage or more than 3 000 800 euros net. Of course, these taxpayers are significantly smaller, around 8 000, so that not only will the revenue in the budget be EUR 40 million, but we estimate somewhere between EUR 10 and 15 million. We also note that the government seems to want to tax the richer by taxing vessels. In fact, it proposes taxation of water vessels in a way that will in fact transfer ties from Slovenian marinas to Croatia and we will not receive an additional 250,000 revenues from our state budget. It is also a matter of what the government wants to achieve with an additional tax on motor vehicles. Richer taxes will try to avoid because instead of buying a new car, they prefer to rent or leasing a car, which is why they will not pay the tax. We think that if the government wanted to tax the rich more, then it would tax the immovable property that cannot be transferred anywhere, it remains in Slovenia and this property certainly includes empty apartments and numerous luxury villas. While a tax on assets worth more than EUR 1 million is proposed under certain conditions, this is far from a tax that could be claimed to be a fair division of the burden of the crisis. The proposed reduction in corporate tax rates from 20% to 18%, including an increase in tax incentives, will also reduce the tax liability for 2012 by 170 million euros. The capital is therefore burdened by €170 million. However, in 2013, 2014 and 2015 following this proposal, the tax rate will be lower by one percentage point each year, which means that in each further year the budget revenue will be lower by an additional EUR 32 million, or in all of these years by 2015 we will have 266 million less in the budget due to a reduction in capital taxation. This is a characteristic of the tax policy of the current government. The saddest part is that the government will spend these 266 million euros by lowering taxes without raising economic growth without eliminating unemployment. For example, if we increased the country's investment for this amount, we would have raised economic growth this year and we could have talked about providing new jobs this year. However, we do note that companies will not invest further, even though their tax liability is lower or their tax relief is increased. In situations where orders are decreasing, companies also reduce production and deplete activity. As a result, no new investments are decided, and an analysis by the Ministry of Finance of the effect of reducing the tax rate on corporate income by means of a general balancing model shows a minimal impact on all elements of the gross social product. In these uncertain situations, we believe that it would be better for the State to directly encourage the economy through subsidies for investment in technological equipment and calls for company development projects. In view of the content of the balance, we find that the rebalancing distorts the subsidisation of enterprises, cuts development resources, and therefore we expect Slovenia to increase the development backlog compared to the European Union, and this is extremely worrying. We think that the overall impact of reducing corporate taxation and reducing investment and subsidies and state aid in the budget will have a negative impact on the recovery of the economy that we know is indebted. We believe that the government should indeed maintain or even increase subsidies to encourage businesses. Over-indebted companies will not recover without tax aid and some will not be able to survive. Banka Slovenije data already show that only one third of companies are able to repay their credit obligations. Reducing investment expenditure and investment transfers in the budget balance will have a very negative impact on economic growth, employment and the existence of certain companies. In doing so, we must pay particular attention to the drastic reduction in the resources for the development of rail infrastructure. In 2012, projects totalling €136.2 million were planned and only €47.3 million is foreseen for the rebalancing, which is a reduction of around €89 million. I think it is clear that without adequate railway infrastructure there will be no economic development. If we make comparisons with the best countries, with better economic prospects, they have higher deficits because these countries are aware of the need to encourage the economy, but not just to save. Austria will, for example, create a high deficit of 3.6 gross social product at 0.6% of economic growth, despite the fact that companies are indebted to the lowest in the European Union. We would also like to stress that the recovery of the economy in the 2009/2011 period could be much faster, but economic growth has further fallen due to the consequences of the wrong economic policy of the then government, Janez Janša, which overheated the economy. Since the beginning of 2005, economic growth has been based on increased indebtedness. The most pronounced increase in gross external debt in 2007 was a good EUR 10 billion, which means that 6.8% of economic growth was based on very unhealthy foundations. The total external gross debt increased from € 15.3 billion to €39.2 billion within the mandate of Mr Janša's previous government. It is said that during the entire mandate of the then government, Slovenia was in charge abroad of almost the same amount as Slovenia owed from independence until the end of 2004. With the return of this government, a rapid consolidation of public finances is proposed in 2012 and in 2013. To a greater extent, the government saves without taking account of the situation in the economy. This will result in a lost decade of government and coalition, as the economy will be picked up much slower than it could otherwise be. We understand the Government's rapid and large-scale strategy of saving Social Democrats by doing this only because they want to be liked by the European Commission and capital owners or financial markets. If we look at the government's direction on expenditure in the proposed rebalancing: if we combine the policy of higher education, science, technology and information society and the policy of entrepreneurship and competitiveness, we find that in 2011 it was earmarked for the developmental conversion of EUR 886 million, 798 000 and 2.84% of gross social product. In 2012, the reduction of funds for this purpose will total 2.13% of the gross social product, or EUR 758 million 376 000, or to the 2008 level. I think it is clear that if we want to reduce the development backlog, we should increase these funds, not reduce them. If the Government would like to look after the economy rather than try to strangle it, it should maintain the level of resources for research and development, investment in education and the level of resources for active employment policy. Unfortunately, government proposals reduce funds for all these measures. With the 2012 proposal for a rebalancing of public finances, together with the proposal for a bill on the balance of public finances for areas within the competence of the Ministry of Education, Science, Culture and Sport, we can conclude that Slovenia is giving up the future that is supposed to be based on knowledge. Compared to the implementation of 2011, the funds are falling by more than €260 million. The reduction of budgetary resources and other measures envisaged by the Government in the field of education, science and research will in the long term reject the intellectual potential of the Slovenian country and the institutions implementing the programmes will be at the brink of survival. Therefore, it is not random to warn experts who compare current events with events in systems that are supposed to be exceeded by civilisation and, in themselves, offer a thesis on the attack on the intellectual core of Slovenian society. It is significant, however, that new, political and economically semi-private higher education institutions are forecasting in the wake of the apparent impoverishment of public education, and it is more or less clear that such thinking is based on government intentions. If we also look at measures, increasing the number of children in classes, closing schools, reducing accessibility to high-quality public education and introducing schooling, impoverishing public universities and other educational institutions, degrading teaching and research professions, they are only part of the measures that the Government of the Republic of Slovenia records in documents and is faithfully sending them to Brussels addresses. It follows clearly from the above that universities and research institutes cannot bear such a drastic reduction in resources mainly in the second half of 2012 and that they will certainly have an emergency situation. "Not paying wages and leaving or preparing for the departure of the best experts will stop the work process, illiquidity will result in the loss of services. All of this will divert attention and action away from teaching and research work with all direct and indirect consequences." This is the thought expressed by the Dean of the Faculty of Economics, Dr. Marble. We also conclude that the government's savings and imbalance in the balance sheet with the Public Finance Balance Act will also jeopardise the functioning of many agencies, institutes and other indirect budgetary users due to the compromises adopted. Unfortunately, the government has decided on a strategy to save and reduce the deficit, which will be the most painful for people, especially for the middle layer. The political recalculation of the government and the failure to implement our rejected structural reforms condemns Slovenia to a lost decade of low economic growth and increasing unemployment. As a result, the Social Democrats cannot support a harmful balance and too quickly and misdirected savings in these two documents. Thank you.
